How many missing BMI values? & \texttt{sum(is.na(nhanes\$BMI))} &
\texttt{nhanes{[}"BMI"{]}.isna().sum()} \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Student Check}\label{student-check}

Before asking AI for any transformation, write down the raw row count,
column count, and missingness count for \texttt{BMI}.

\chapter{Python Tracebacks}\label{python-tracebacks}

\section{The Bottom Line Is Usually At The
Bottom}\label{the-bottom-line-is-usually-at-the-bottom}

Python errors often print several lines called a traceback. Read from
the bottom upward:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: \textquotesingle{}Data/patients.csv\textquotesingle{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This tells you the immediate problem: Python cannot find the file at the
path you gave it.

\section{Common Week 5 Errors}\label{common-week-5-errors}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Error
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Likely cause
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
First fix
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{FileNotFoundError} & path is wrong, file is in another folder,
or capitalization differs & check the relative path from the notebook
location \\
\texttt{ModuleNotFoundError} & package is missing & add the package to
\texttt{requirements.txt} and install it in the Codespace \\
\texttt{KeyError} & column name is misspelled or not present & run
\texttt{nhanes.columns.tolist()} \\
\texttt{IndentationError} & Python block spacing is inconsistent &
re-indent the block with four spaces \\
\texttt{NameError} & object has not been created in the current kernel &
rerun earlier cells in order \\
\end{longtable}

\section{AI Debugging Prompt}\label{ai-debugging-prompt}

\begin{quote}
Read this Python traceback from the bottom up. Identify the error type,
the exact object or path causing the issue, and the smallest code change
that would fix it. Do not rewrite the full analysis.
\end{quote}

\section{Student Check}\label{student-check-1}

When you fix an error, add one sentence to your notebook explaining what
failed and how you knew the fix worked.

\chapter{One Step, One Cell}\label{one-step-one-cell}

\section{Why This Rule Exists}\label{why-this-rule-exists}

Large AI-generated code blocks are hard to audit. A single hidden
mistake in loading, filtering, grouping, or missingness handling can
cascade into a polished but incorrect result.

Use one cell per analytic step:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  load data;
\item
  inspect rows and columns;
\item
  filter missing values;
\item
  group and summarise;
\item
  compare with the R result.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Good AI Prompt}\label{good-ai-prompt}

\begin{quote}
Translate only the data-loading step from R to Python/pandas. Tell me
what output I should inspect before moving to the next step.
\end{quote}

\section{Risky AI Prompt}\label{risky-ai-prompt}

\begin{quote}
Translate my full analysis, make the plots, and write the
interpretation.
\end{quote}

The risky prompt gives up too much control. It may change the analytic
question without telling you.

\section{Parity Audit Checks}\label{parity-audit-checks}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Check
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
R
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Python
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Row count & \texttt{nrow(df)} & \texttt{len(df)} or
\texttt{df.shape{[}0{]}} \\
Column names & \texttt{names(df)} & \texttt{df.columns.tolist()} \\
Unique levels & \texttt{unique(df\$IncomeGroup)} &
\texttt{df{[}"IncomeGroup"{]}.unique()} \\
Missing values & \texttt{sum(is.na(df\$BMI))} &
\texttt{df{[}"BMI"{]}.isna().sum()} \\
Summary table rows & \texttt{nrow(summary\_df)} &
\texttt{len(summary\_df)} \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Notebook Habit}\label{notebook-habit}

After each Python cell, add a short Markdown note:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Audit check: this cell matches the R output because ...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you cannot fill in that sentence, do not move to the next cell yet.

\chapter{Mixing R and Python}\label{mixing-r-and-python}

\section{Why Interoperability
Matters}\label{why-interoperability-matters}

Sometimes a project has one useful Python tool, but the rest of the
analysis is already in R. The \texttt{reticulate} package lets R call
Python code and bring Python objects back into the R session. In this
course, \texttt{reticulate} is awareness-level: you should know what it
does and when it might be useful, but you are not expected to build a
full mixed-language pipeline.

\section{Minimal Demo}\label{minimal-demo}

Run this in an R console or an R Quarto document if \texttt{reticulate}
is available in your Codespace.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(reticulate)}

\FunctionTok{py\_run\_string}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}
\StringTok{import pandas as pd}
\StringTok{numbers = pd.DataFrame(\{\textquotesingle{}group\textquotesingle{}: [\textquotesingle{}A\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}A\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}B\textquotesingle{}], \textquotesingle{}value\textquotesingle{}: [10, 15, 20]\})}
\StringTok{summary = numbers.groupby(\textquotesingle{}group\textquotesingle{}, as\_index=False)[\textquotesingle{}value\textquotesingle{}].mean()}
\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{py}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{summary}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Health Data Example}\label{health-data-example}

The same idea works with the NHANES case-study CSV:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(reticulate)}

\FunctionTok{py\_run\_string}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}
\StringTok{import pandas as pd}
\StringTok{nhanes = pd.read\_csv(\textquotesingle{}../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv\textquotesingle{})}
\StringTok{bmi\_rows = nhanes[[\textquotesingle{}BMI\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}IncomeGroup\textquotesingle{}]].dropna()}
\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{bmi\_rows }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ py}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bmi\_rows}
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(bmi\_rows)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{When To Use It}\label{when-to-use-it}

Use \texttt{reticulate} when:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a small Python library solves a specific problem better than the R
  ecosystem;
\item
  the rest of your report, tables, or dashboard are already in R;
\item
  you can document the Python dependency clearly.
\end{itemize}

Avoid it when:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a clean R solution already exists;
\item
  mixing languages makes the project harder to reproduce;
\item
  the Python step cannot run in a fresh Codespace.
\end{itemize}

\section{Check Your Understanding}\label{check-your-understanding-1}

In one sentence, explain what object moves from Python back into R in
the NHANES example above.

\chapter{Reusable Python Scripts}\label{reusable-python-scripts}

\section{Notebook or Script?}\label{notebook-or-script}

Use a notebook when you are exploring, explaining, and checking outputs.
Use a script when you have a helper function that should be reused
without copying and pasting.

This mirrors the R pattern from Week 3: once a chunk of code has a clear
job, turn it into a named function.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Create
\texttt{helpers.py}}{Create helpers.py}}\label{create-helpers.py}

In the Week 5 folder, create a file named \texttt{helpers.py} with this
function:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ pathlib }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Path}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ pandas }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ pd}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ load\_and\_preview(path, n\_rows}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{):}
    \CommentTok{"""Load a CSV, print its shape, and return the first rows."""}
\NormalTok{    csv\_path }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Path(path)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ csv\_path.exists():}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{FileNotFoundError}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Could not find file: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{csv\_path}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{    data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pd.read\_csv(csv\_path)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Loaded }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{data}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ rows and }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{data}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{shape[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ columns"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ data.head(n\_rows)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Import It In A Notebook}\label{import-it-in-a-notebook}

In \texttt{translation.ipynb}, import and use the helper:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ helpers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ load\_and\_preview}

\NormalTok{preview }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ load\_and\_preview(}\StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{preview}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Reproducibility Check}\label{reproducibility-check-1}

Before committing:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  verify that \texttt{helpers.py} is saved in the same folder as your
  notebook;
\item
  rerun the notebook from a fresh kernel;
\item
  check that the relative path starts from the notebook location;
\item
  commit both files together with a message such as
  \texttt{Add\ Week\ 5\ Python\ helper}.
\end{itemize}

\section{Student Output}\label{student-output-1}

For Assignment 4, your reusable script should contain at least one
function that is actually called from the notebook. A function that is
written but never used does not demonstrate reproducibility.

\chapter{Git Progress}\label{git-progress}

\section{Commit The Audit Trail}\label{commit-the-audit-trail}

Week 4 introduced Git as analysis provenance. Week 5 uses that skill to
make translation auditable.

Commit after meaningful checkpoints:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  R pipeline works.
\item
  R function reproduces the pipeline.
\item
  Python notebook loads the same data.
\item
  Python summary matches the R summary.
\item
  Final parity note is complete.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Notebook Output Hygiene}\label{notebook-output-hygiene}

Jupyter notebooks can store rendered tables and images inside the
\texttt{.ipynb} file. That makes Git diffs noisy.

Before committing:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open the Command Palette with \texttt{Ctrl+Shift+P}.
\item
  Run \texttt{Notebook:\ Clear\ All\ Outputs}.
\item
  Save the notebook.
\item
  Stage \texttt{translation.ipynb}, \texttt{helpers.py}, and your Quarto
  note together.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Example Commit Messages}\label{example-commit-messages}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Add R BMI summary for Week 5 parity check}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Translate BMI summary to pandas and verify row counts}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Complete Assignment 4 parity audit note}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Student Check}\label{student-check-2}

Your Git history should show the work developing in steps, not one
unexplained final upload.

\chapter{Proposal Readiness}\label{proposal-readiness}

\section{Why This Belongs In Week 5}\label{why-this-belongs-in-week-5}

Before the project proposal is due, you should know whether your chosen
dataset can be loaded, inspected, and explained in the course workflow.
Week 5 adds one extra test: can you verify the basic data shape in both
R and Python?

\section{Proposal Readiness
Checklist}\label{proposal-readiness-checklist}

Your proposal should include:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a focused health-data question;
\item
  the dataset name and source;
\item
  a brief provenance and stewardship note;
\item
  a feasibility check showing the file can load in a Codespace;
\item
  one or two planned descriptive summaries or visualizations;
\item
  any known privacy, licensing, or access limitations.
\end{itemize}

\section{Feasibility Check}\label{feasibility-check}

For your project dataset, record:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Check & What to write \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
File path & relative path from the repo root \\
File format & CSV, RDS, Excel, API, or other \\
Row and column count & basic shape after loading \\
Key variables & names and meanings of the fields you expect to use \\
Missingness concern & one variable where missing values may matter \\
R/Python check & whether both languages can load the file \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Student Output}\label{student-output-2}

Add this feasibility check to your project proposal notes. If your
dataset does not load cleanly yet, describe the blocker honestly instead
of hiding it.

\chapter{Knowledge Check}\label{knowledge-check-2}

Answer these in a Markdown cell or short Quarto note.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Why is Week 5 called ``polyglot awareness'' rather than ``Python
  replacement''?
\item
  Name one task where R is the better default for this course.
\item
  Name one task where Python might be useful.
\item
  Why does \texttt{my\_list{[}1{]}} return the second item in Python?
\item
  What is the difference between \texttt{=} and \texttt{==}?
\item
  What does \texttt{requirements.txt} document?
\item
  What is the One Step, One Cell rule?
\item
  What are three parity checks you should run after translating R code
  to Python?
\item
  Why should notebook outputs be cleared before committing?
\item
  What does \texttt{reticulate} allow R to do?
\end{enumerate}

\section{Short Applied Check}\label{short-applied-check}

Given the R expression:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(nhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{BMI, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Write the Python/pandas equivalent and name the missingness check you
would run before trusting it.

\chapter{Bilingual Translation}\label{bilingual-translation}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-1}

This exercise turns the week into a concrete artifact: one small R
analysis, one Python translation, and one audit note showing that the
outputs agree.

\section{Required Task}\label{required-task}

Translate the Week 5 R-deepening summary into Python/pandas:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Start from the R pipeline in \hyperref[r-deepening-and-parity-check]{R
  Deepening and Parity Check}.
\item
  Create \texttt{translation.ipynb}.
\item
  Load the NHANES CSV from
  \texttt{../../examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}.
\item
  Recreate the BMI-by-income-and-gender summary in Python.
\item
  Compare the Python output to the R output using row counts, grouping
  labels, and rounded summary values.
\item
  Extract one Python helper into \texttt{helpers.py} and call it from
  the notebook.
\item
  Add a Markdown cell explaining what AI helped with and how you
  verified the result.
\item
  Clear notebook outputs before committing.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Parity Table Template}\label{parity-table-template}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrrcl@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Check & R result & Python result & Match? & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Raw row count & & & & \\
Filtered row count & & & & \\
Number of grouped rows & & & & \\
Mean BMI values after rounding & & & & \\
Group labels & & & & \\
\end{longtable}

\section{AI Prompt}\label{ai-prompt}

\begin{quote}
I have a working R \texttt{dplyr} summary. Translate it to Python/pandas
one step at a time. After each step, tell me exactly what output to
compare with R. Do not add new variables, filters, plots, or
interpretation.
\end{quote}

\section{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-1}

Your exercise is complete when a reader can open the notebook, rerun the
cells in order, and see why the R and Python outputs do or do not match.

\chapter{R To Python Cheatsheet}\label{r-to-python-cheatsheet}

Use this as a translation aid, not as a substitute for checking outputs.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Task
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
R
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Python
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Load package & \texttt{library(dplyr)} &
\texttt{import\ pandas\ as\ pd} \\
Read CSV & \texttt{read\_csv("file.csv")} &
\texttt{pd.read\_csv("file.csv")} \\
First rows & \texttt{head(df)} & \texttt{df.head()} \\
Row count & \texttt{nrow(df)} & \texttt{len(df)} or
\texttt{df.shape{[}0{]}} \\
Column names & \texttt{names(df)} & \texttt{df.columns.tolist()} \\
Select columns & \texttt{select(df,\ BMI,\ Gender)} &
\texttt{df{[}{[}"BMI",\ "Gender"{]}{]}} \\
Filter rows & \texttt{filter(df,\ Age\ \textgreater{}=\ 20)} &
\texttt{df{[}df{[}"Age"{]}\ \textgreater{}=\ 20{]}} \\
Missing values & \texttt{sum(is.na(df\$BMI))} &
\texttt{df{[}"BMI"{]}.isna().sum()} \\
Group summary &
\texttt{group\_by(...)\ \textbar{}\textgreater{}\ summarise(...)} &
\texttt{.groupby(...).agg(...)} \\
New column & \texttt{mutate(df,\ whtr\ =\ Waist\ /\ Height)} &
\texttt{df{[}"whtr"{]}\ =\ df{[}"Waist"{]}\ /\ df{[}"Height"{]}} \\
Sort rows & \texttt{arrange(df,\ IncomeGroup)} &
\texttt{df.sort\_values("IncomeGroup")} \\
Unique values & \texttt{unique(df\$Gender)} &
\texttt{df{[}"Gender"{]}.unique()} \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Translation Rule}\label{translation-rule}

The translation is not finished when the code runs. It is finished when
the R and Python outputs have been compared and the comparison is
documented.

\part{Week 6: Data Visualization}

\chapter*{Visual Storytelling}\label{visual-storytelling}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Visual Storytelling}

\markboth{Visual Storytelling}{Visual Storytelling}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This chapter challenges students to move beyond default charts and
create publication-quality visualizations. You will use AI tools to
generate initial code for complex plots in ggplot2 or plotly, and then
apply human judgment to refine them for clarity, accuracy, and
aesthetics. The lesson emphasizes detecting misleading AI-generated
visuals and ``steering'' the code to tell a truthful and compelling data
story.

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 5 checked that summaries agree across languages. This week turns a
descriptive summary into a visual claim and asks whether the design
helps or misleads the audience. Next week you will document the same
dataset with EDA and a Table 1.

\section*{Case Study Data Analysis}\label{case-study-data-analysis-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Case Study Data Analysis}

\markright{Case Study Data Analysis}

The \textbf{NHANES Health Equity data spine} now supports code-reading,
EDA, visualization, and reproducibility checks. Use the cached CSV/RDS
for routine class work; a CDC retrieval script exists for advanced
users.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Cached RDS:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.rds}
\item
  CSV snapshot:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Case-study README: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/README.md}
\end{itemize}

The default classroom path is to use the cached data so the analysis
work is reproducible without internet access.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{rows =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_income =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup))}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 1 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}     rows missing\_bmi missing\_income}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}int\textgreater{}       \textless{}int\textgreater{}          \textless{}int\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 105626        9356           9415}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup), Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 3 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5) 16330     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 2 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)  15293     29.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 3 Middle Income      19228     29.3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\chapter*{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI
Visualization}\label{worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI
Visualization}

\markboth{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI Visualization}{Worked Example:
Descriptive BMI Visualization}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This worked example uses the NHANES Health Equity CSV snapshot to build
one descriptive visualization. The plot summarizes mean BMI by income
group across NHANES cycle. It is \textbf{unweighted} and should be
interpreted as a classroom visualization exercise, not a population
estimate.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Prepare a Descriptive
Summary}\label{prepare-a-descriptive-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Prepare a Descriptive Summary}

\markright{Prepare a Descriptive Summary}

Keep the summary intentionally simple. We are describing the prepared
class dataset, not estimating a national trend.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup),}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Cycle),}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Cycle, IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 33 x 4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    Cycle     IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}chr\textgreater{}     \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  1 1999{-}2000 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1123     27.9}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  2 1999{-}2000 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1091     28.8}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  3 1999{-}2000 Middle Income       1327     29.0}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  4 2001{-}2002 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1491     28.1}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  5 2001{-}2002 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1089     28.7}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  6 2001{-}2002 Middle Income       1561     28.3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  7 2003{-}2004 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1284     28.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  8 2003{-}2004 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1183     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  9 2003{-}2004 Middle Income       1573     28.8}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 10 2005{-}2006 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1480     28.5}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# i 23 more rows}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Plot}\label{plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Plot}

\markright{Plot}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{  bmi\_by\_income\_cycle,}
  \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Cycle, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ mean\_bmi, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup, }\AttributeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup)}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI by income group across NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Adults age 20{-}80 in the prepared class dataset; unweighted descriptive summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Income group"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Caption template: Mean BMI is summarized descriptively from the cached class dataset. Values are unweighted, exclude missing BMI/income/cycle values, and should not be interpreted as causal effects or population estimates."}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{base\_size =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{axis.text.x =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{angle =} \DecValTok{45}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{hjust =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"bottom"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{weeks/week06-visualization/worked-example-ggplot_files/figure-pdf/plot-bmi-by-income-cycle-1.pdf}}

\section*{Caption Template}\label{caption-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Caption Template}

\markright{Caption Template}

Use this structure when revising your own plot:

\begin{quote}
This figure shows {[}measure{]} by {[}group{]} across
{[}time/category{]} using the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom
dataset. The summary is descriptive and unweighted. Records with missing
{[}variables{]} were excluded. The plot supports comparison of patterns,
not causal claims or population-level estimates.
\end{quote}

\section*{Mini Visual Audit
Checklist}\label{mini-visual-audit-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Mini Visual Audit Checklist}

\markright{Mini Visual Audit Checklist}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Does the title describe what is plotted without overstating the claim?
\item
  Is it clear whether the statistic is weighted or unweighted?
\item
  Are axes, groups, and units labeled plainly?
\item
  Are missing values or exclusions disclosed?
\item
  Is the scale chosen to support honest comparison rather than
  exaggeration?
\item
  Does the caption state the limitation that this is descriptive, not
  causal?
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Activity: AI Visual Audit}\label{activity-ai-visual-audit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Activity: AI Visual Audit}

\markboth{Activity: AI Visual Audit}{Activity: AI Visual Audit}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Students evaluate AI-generated visualizations for correctness, clarity,
bias, aggregation choices, and misleading design.

\section*{Inputs}\label{inputs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Inputs}

\markright{Inputs}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Student worked example:
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/worked-example-ggplot.qmd}
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Flawed plot/code: provided by your instructor or TA during the
  activity
\end{itemize}

\section*{Activity Steps}\label{activity-steps}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Activity Steps}

\markright{Activity Steps}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Review the worked example and note how it labels the data source,
  statistic, and limitation.
\item
  Inspect the flawed plot or code provided by the instructor.
\item
  Identify at least three issues that could mislead a reader.
\item
  Revise the plot so the title, scale, grouping, and caption match the
  evidence.
\item
  Write a short note explaining what changed and why.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Required Outputs}\label{required-outputs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Required Outputs}

\markright{Required Outputs}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{visual-audit-note.md}: an audit note listing at least three
  issues.
\item
  \texttt{corrected-plot.qmd}: a rerunnable Quarto file that creates the
  corrected plot.
\item
  \texttt{corrected-plot.png}: the exported corrected plot.
\item
  \texttt{caption.md}: a caption that states the statistic, data source,
  exclusions, and limitations.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md}: if you used AI, explain what it helped with
  and how you checked the result.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Audit Categories}\label{audit-categories}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Audit Categories}

\markright{Audit Categories}

Use these categories in your audit note:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Correctness:} Does the plot show what the title and labels
  claim?
\item
  \textbf{Design:} Do scale, color, ordering, and geometry support fair
  comparison?
\item
  \textbf{Interpretation:} Does the caption avoid causal or
  population-level claims that the plot cannot support?
\item
  \textbf{Reproducibility:} Can another person rerun the code from the
  repository root using relative paths?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Completion Check}\label{completion-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Completion Check}

\markright{Completion Check}

Your submission is complete when a peer can rerun your corrected plot,
identify the main comparison without reading your code, and see at least
one explicit limitation in the caption.

\part{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI Auditing}

\chapter*{Exploring Data}\label{exploring-data}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Exploring Data}

\markboth{Exploring Data}{Exploring Data}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

Before modeling, you must understand your data. This chapter guides
students through the rigorous process of Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA)
and the creation of the standard ``Table 1'' for demographic
characteristics. You will learn to document every cleaning decision and
exclusion criteria in a reproducible Quarto log, ensuring that your path
from raw data to analysis dataset is transparent and repeatable by
anyone.

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 6 focused on whether visual design makes a descriptive claim honest
and clear. This week documents the analysis cohort, missingness, and
Table 1 so that later dashboard and report outputs have an auditable
foundation.

\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definition of Done}

\chapter*{Worked Example: EDA and Table
1}\label{worked-example-eda-and-table-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Worked Example: EDA and Table 1}

\markboth{Worked Example: EDA and Table 1}{Worked Example: EDA and Table
1}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This worked example creates a small Table 1-style descriptive summary
from the NHANES Health Equity CSV snapshot. The goal is transparent EDA:
define the cohort, check missingness, summarize key variables, and write
a methods note that does not overclaim.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Define the Analysis Cohort}\label{define-the-analysis-cohort}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Define the Analysis Cohort}

\markright{Define the Analysis Cohort}

Decision: restrict to adults age 20-80. This keeps the example focused
on adult BMI summaries and avoids mixing children, adolescents, and the
oldest age-coded records into one classroom table.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{analysis\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{cohort\_counts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{step =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Prepared CSV rows"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Rows after age 20{-}80 restriction"}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(nhanes), }\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df))}
\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(cohort\_counts)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
step & n \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Prepared CSV rows & 105626 \\
Rows after age 20-80 restriction & 57137 \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Missingness Check}\label{missingness-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Missingness Check}

\markright{Missingness Check}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{key\_vars }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"BMI"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Age"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"IncomeGroup"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Gender"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Race"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Cycle"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{missingness }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{variable =}\NormalTok{ key\_vars) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_n =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df[key\_vars], }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(x)), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_percent =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ missing\_n }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missingness)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
variable & missing\_n & missing\_percent \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
BMI & 1009 & 1.8 \\
Age & 0 & 0.0 \\
IncomeGroup & 5395 & 9.4 \\
Gender & 0 & 0.0 \\
Race & 0 & 0.0 \\
Cycle & 0 & 0.0 \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Table 1-Style Summary}\label{table-1-style-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Table 1-Style Summary}

\markright{Table 1-Style Summary}

This example stratifies by \texttt{IncomeGroup} and reports N plus mean
(SD) for BMI and age. It is unweighted and descriptive.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{mean\_sd }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) \{}
  \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%.1f (\%.1f)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{table1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ analysis\_df }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{N =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{BMI, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean\_sd}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Age, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean\_sd}\NormalTok{(Age),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(table1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
IncomeGroup & N & BMI, mean (SD) & Age, mean (SD) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
High Income (\textgreater3.5) & 16520 & 28.6 (6.3) & 49.7 (16.0) \\
Low Income (\textless1.3) & 15646 & 29.4 (7.5) & 47.4 (18.1) \\
Middle Income & 19576 & 29.3 (7.0) & 50.0 (18.4) \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Methods Note Template}\label{methods-note-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Methods Note Template}

\markright{Methods Note Template}

\begin{quote}
We used the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom CSV snapshot. The
analysis cohort included records with age 20-80. We summarized BMI and
age by income group using unweighted descriptive statistics. Missing
values were checked before summarizing. These summaries describe the
prepared class dataset and should not be interpreted as causal effects
or population estimates.
\end{quote}

\section*{Reproducibility Notes}\label{reproducibility-notes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reproducibility Notes}

\markright{Reproducibility Notes}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The data path is relative to the repository.
\item
  The cohort restriction is stated before the table.
\item
  Missingness is reported before complete-case summaries.
\item
  The table labels distinguish N, mean, and SD.
\item
  The methods note states that the analysis is descriptive and
  non-causal.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Activity: Table 1 and AI
Audit}\label{activity-table-1-and-ai-audit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Activity: Table 1 and AI Audit}

\markboth{Activity: Table 1 and AI Audit}{Activity: Table 1 and AI
Audit}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Students create a descriptive summary and audit AI-generated code or
interpretation for planted errors.

\section*{Inputs}\label{inputs-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Inputs}

\markright{Inputs}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Student worked example:
  \texttt{weeks/week07-eda-ai-audit/worked-example-table1.qmd}
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Planted-error starter: distributed by the instructor or TA during the
  activity
\end{itemize}

\section*{Activity Steps}\label{activity-steps-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Activity Steps}

\markright{Activity Steps}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Review the worked example and identify the cohort definition,
  missingness check, and Table 1 labels.
\item
  Open the planted-error starter provided by the instructor.
\item
  Run or read the starter code and annotate at least five issues.
\item
  Categorize each issue as \textbf{correctness},
  \textbf{reproducibility}, or \textbf{interpretation}.
\item
  Produce a corrected EDA note and Table 1.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Required Outputs}\label{required-outputs-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Required Outputs}

\markright{Required Outputs}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{eda-note.qmd}: a rerunnable Quarto note with cohort
  definition, missingness table, and Table 1.
\item
  \texttt{eda-note.html}: rendered output from the Quarto note.
\item
  \texttt{table1.csv}: exported Table 1 or equivalent descriptive
  summary.
\item
  \texttt{audit-note.md}: at least five detected issues with category,
  why it matters, and corrected approach.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md}: if you used AI, explain what it helped with
  and how you verified the result.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Minimum Table 1
Requirements}\label{minimum-table-1-requirements}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Minimum Table 1 Requirements}

\markright{Minimum Table 1 Requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Cohort: age 20-80, unless your instructor approves a different cohort.
\item
  Stratifier: \texttt{IncomeGroup}, unless your instructor assigns a
  different grouping variable.
\item
  Variables: N, BMI mean (SD), and age mean (SD).
\item
  Methods note: state that the table is descriptive, unweighted, and
  non-causal.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Completion Check}\label{completion-check-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Completion Check}

\markright{Completion Check}

Your submission is complete when another student can rerun your
\texttt{eda-note.qmd}, see exactly who is included, understand how
missingness was handled, and identify why the planted-error starter was
misleading.

\part{Week 8: Dashboard Prototypes for KT}

\chapter*{Interactive Dashboards}\label{interactive-dashboards}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Interactive Dashboards}

\markboth{Interactive Dashboards}{Interactive Dashboards}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

Static PDF reports often sit unread, so this chapter introduces dynamic
Knowledge Translation through Shiny apps and dashboards. Students will
take their prior EDA results and transform them into interactive web
tools that allow users to filter and explore the data themselves. You
will learn the best practices of user interface design for health data
and deploy your first live dashboard to the web via GitHub Pages or
Shinylive.

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 7 established an auditable descriptive foundation. This week is the
first app-forward week: students run the NHANES dashboard, identify an
audience, and improve one audience-facing element. Next week turns one
dashboard finding into a short scientific communication product.

\section*{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES
Equity}\label{dashboard-case-study-nhanes-equity}

\markright{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES Equity}

The \textbf{NHANES Health Equity Dashboard} is now the foreground case
study. Use it as a small knowledge-translation product built on the
cached NHANES equity dataset.

\item

\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}

\section*{In-Class Activity}\label{in-class-activity-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{In-Class Activity}

\markright{In-Class Activity}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Run the app and describe the intended audience in one sentence.
\item
  Make one audience-facing improvement to a label, help text, or
  interpretation note.
\item
  Verify the app still loads the cached RDS after your change.
\item
  Write one limitation statement that belongs inside or beside the
  dashboard.
\end{itemize}

Student output: a local app run note, one audience-facing text
improvement, and a limitation statement. Assignment link:
\texttt{../../assignments/assignment07-dashboard/README.md}.

\section*{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definition of Done}

\markright{Definition of Done}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Students can produce a small dashboard-style KT artifact from prior
  EDA outputs.
\item
  The dashboard runs or renders in the supported workflow.
\item
  The page distinguishes required core work from optional deployment
  extensions.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Starter Dashboard}\label{starter-dashboard}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Starter Dashboard}

\markboth{Starter Dashboard}{Starter Dashboard}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Students adapt a minimal dashboard-style knowledge translation product
by changing text and interpretation before changing analytic logic.

\section*{Required Elements}\label{required-elements}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Required Elements}

\markright{Required Elements}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  One audience-specific question.
\item
  One interactive or dashboard-style control.
\item
  One visualization.
\item
  One plain-language interpretation.
\item
  One limitation or caution.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Minimal Review Workflow}\label{minimal-review-workflow}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Minimal Review Workflow}

\markright{Minimal Review Workflow}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Run the NHANES dashboard from the repository root.
\item
  Choose a stakeholder audience, such as a public-health analyst,
  student team, or community-facing briefing group.
\item
  Identify one label, caption, help text, or interpretation note that
  could be clearer for that audience.
\item
  Make a documentation-only or UI-text-only change.
\item
  Rerun the dashboard and verify the cached dataset loads.
\item
  Record the change, audience, and limitation in a short note.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Student Output}\label{student-output-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Student Output}

\markright{Student Output}

Submit a short dashboard adaptation note containing:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Audience sentence.
\item
  Before/after text change.
\item
  One screenshot or description of the affected dashboard area.
\item
  Limitation statement.
\item
  Reproducibility note describing how the app was run locally.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Privacy-Safe Publishing
Check}\label{privacy-safe-publishing-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Privacy-Safe Publishing Check}

\markright{Privacy-Safe Publishing Check}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Do not publish row-level data.
\item
  Avoid small-cell claims.
\item
  State that dashboard outputs are descriptive.
\item
  Keep the cached classroom data separate from raw NHANES retrieval
  work.
\end{itemize}

\part{Week 9: Communication of Scientific Findings}

\chapter*{Dynamic Presentations}\label{dynamic-presentations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Dynamic Presentations}

\markboth{Dynamic Presentations}{Dynamic Presentations}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

Data analysis is useless if it cannot be understood by key stakeholders.
This chapter focuses on ``Audience-Specific KT,'' teaching you to
convert technical findings into plain language summaries and engaging
slide decks using Quarto. You will practice peer review and learn to use
LLMs to simplify jargon without losing scientific accuracy, ensuring
your work can impact policy and practice.

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 8 made the dashboard audience-facing. This week turns one dashboard
plot or table into a short slide story with a clear takeaway, evidence,
and limitation. Next week you will expand that communication into a
reproducible report.

\chapter*{Revealjs and Peer Feedback}\label{revealjs-and-peer-feedback}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Revealjs and Peer Feedback}

\markboth{Revealjs and Peer Feedback}{Revealjs and Peer Feedback}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Students turn one dashboard finding into a short scientific
communication product and practice structured peer feedback.

\section*{Minimal Revealjs Starter}\label{minimal-revealjs-starter}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Minimal Revealjs Starter}

\markright{Minimal Revealjs Starter}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\PreprocessorTok{{-}{-}{-}}
\FunctionTok{title}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ }\StringTok{"NHANES Equity Dashboard Finding"}
\FunctionTok{format}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ revealjs}
\PreprocessorTok{{-}{-}{-}}
\end{Highlighting}

\section*{Three-Slide Structure}\label{three-slide-structure}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three-Slide Structure}

\markright{Three-Slide Structure}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Question and audience:} Who needs this finding, and why?
\item
  \textbf{Evidence:} Show one dashboard plot or table and state the
  descriptive pattern.
\item
  \textbf{Limitation and action:} Name what the data can and cannot
  support, then state the next decision or question.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Peer Feedback Form}\label{peer-feedback-form}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Peer Feedback Form}

\markright{Peer Feedback Form}

\toprule\noalign{}
Prompt & Peer note \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
What is the main message? & \\
What evidence supports it? & \\
What is unclear or overclaimed? & \\
Is the limitation visible? & \\
What would improve audience fit? & \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Student Output}\label{student-output-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Student Output}

\markright{Student Output}

feedback form. Include a source note for CDC NHANES and an AI-use note
if AI helped draft the wording.

\part{Week 10: Writing \& Publishing Reports}

\chapter*{Reproducible Reporting}\label{reproducible-reporting}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Reproducible Reporting}

\markboth{Reproducible Reporting}{Reproducible Reporting}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This chapter simulates the final stretch of a research project: writing
the manuscript. Students learn to assemble their analysis, code, and
writing into a single ``dynamic report'' that automatically updates
numbers and figures if the data changes. You will explore advanced
publishing workflows to create professional, citation-ready portfolios
that demonstrate your ability to execute end-to-end data science.

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 9 turned one result into a short slide story. This week expands the
same evidence into a reproducible report with code-generated outputs,
citations, and limitations. Next week you will stress-test whether the
report and dashboard work in a fresh environment.

\chapter*{Report Template: NHANES Equity
Summary}\label{report-template-nhanes-equity-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Report Template: NHANES Equity Summary}

\markboth{Report Template: NHANES Equity Summary}{Report Template:
NHANES Equity Summary}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

This runnable template shows how to pair a dashboard with a short
reproducible report. The dashboard supports exploration; the report
fixes one question, documents the data, and cites the source.

\section*{Question}\label{question}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Question}

\markright{Question}

How does mean BMI vary by income group among adults age 20-80 in the
prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom dataset?

\section*{Data Source}\label{data-source}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Data Source}

\markright{Data Source}

The report uses the cached CSV snapshot at
\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}. The source
data are derived from CDC/NCHS NHANES public-use files (Centers for
Disease Control and Prevention, National Center for Health Statistics
2026).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Methods}\label{methods}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Methods}

\markright{Methods}

We restricted the classroom dataset to adults age 20-80 and summarized
BMI by income group. The summaries are unweighted and descriptive. They
should not be interpreted as causal effects or national population
estimates.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{analysis\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{missingness }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ analysis\_df }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{rows =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_income =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_age =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Age))}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missingness, }\AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Missingness check for key report variables."}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rrrr@{}}
\caption{Missingness check for key report variables.}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
rows & missing\_bmi & missing\_income & missing\_age \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
rows & missing\_bmi & missing\_income & missing\_age \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot

\section*{Limitations}\label{limitations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Limitations}

\markright{Limitations}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The table and figure use a prepared classroom dataset rather than raw
  NHANES files.
\item
  The summaries are unweighted.
\item
  The comparison is descriptive and does not establish causality.
\item
  Missing BMI or income group values are excluded from the grouped
  summary.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Plain-Language Summary}\label{plain-language-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Plain-Language Summary}

\markright{Plain-Language Summary}

In this classroom dataset, mean BMI differs across income groups. The
result is useful for practicing reproducible reporting and dashboard
interpretation, but it should be described as a descriptive pattern
rather than a causal finding.

\section*{AI-Use and Audit Note}\label{ai-use-and-audit-note}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{AI-Use and Audit Note}

\markright{AI-Use and Audit Note}

Document any AI assistance used to draft code, captions, or
interpretation. State exactly which output you checked against the code,
data dictionary, or rendered report.

\part{Week 11: Portfolio Surgery}

\chapter*{Advanced Topics}\label{advanced-topics}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Advanced Topics}

\markboth{Advanced Topics}{Advanced Topics}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

Before the final submission, students must ``bulletproof'' their work.
This chapter focuses on advanced project hygiene, dependency management
(ensuring your code runs on someone else's machine), and auditability.
You will act as a peer reviewer to identify reproducibility failures in
others' code, fixing broken paths and missing seeds, to ensure your
final project stands up to scientific scrutiny.

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 10 produced a report with code-generated outputs and citations.
This week tests whether the report, dashboard, and instructions survive
a fresh environment. The final portfolio should leave a reviewer able to
rerun the core work without private setup knowledge.

\section*{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES
Equity}\label{dashboard-case-study-nhanes-equity-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES Equity}

\markright{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES Equity}

The \textbf{NHANES Health Equity Dashboard} is now the foreground case
study. Use it as a small knowledge-translation product built on the
cached NHANES equity dataset.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  App code: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/app/app.R}
\item
  Cached data:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.rds} and
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Optional rebuild script:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/scripts/build\_nhanes\_equity.R}
\end{itemize}

Run the dashboard from the repository root:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{shiny}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{runApp}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/app"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The app is offline-first: it loads the local RDS cache when available.
Rebuilding from CDC uses \texttt{nhanesA}, requires internet access, and
is optional unless assigned.

Companion materials:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Week 10 report template:
  \texttt{weeks/week10-reporting-publishing/report-template.qmd}
\item
  Week 11 static dashboard demo:
  \texttt{weeks/week11-portfolio-surgery/static-dashboard-ojs.qmd}
\end{itemize}

\section*{In-Class Activity}\label{in-class-activity-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{In-Class Activity}

\markright{In-Class Activity}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Run a fresh-session test: can the app launch from the repository root?
\item
  Verify the cached RDS path works before attempting a CDC rebuild.
\item
  Optionally run the rebuild script only if internet access and time are
  available.
\item
  Optionally draft a static dashboard extension using a CSV snapshot and
  no Shiny server.
\item
  Log any dependency, path, or README issues as reproducibility risks.
\end{itemize}

Student output: a reproducibility test log, repaired README or path
issues, and a final risk list. Assignment link:
\texttt{../../assignments/assignment10-portfolio-surgery/README.md}.

\section*{Static Dashboard Demo}\label{static-dashboard-demo}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Static Dashboard Demo}

\markright{Static Dashboard Demo}

The completed \href{static-dashboard-ojs.qmd}{static dashboard source
file} demonstrates a no-server alternative that reads the NHANES CSV
snapshot in the browser. Use it as an exemplar for optional portfolio
extensions after the core Shiny and report workflows are reproducible.

\section*{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definition of Done}

\markright{Definition of Done}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A peer can clone or open the repository in a fresh Codespace and
  reproduce the core outputs.
\item
  Broken paths, missing dependencies, unclear READMEs, and ungrounded AI
  claims are fixed.
\item
  The final portfolio checklist is linked from this page.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Reproducibility Stress
Test}\label{reproducibility-stress-test}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Reproducibility Stress Test}

\markboth{Reproducibility Stress Test}{Reproducibility Stress Test}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Students test whether a project can run in a fresh environment and
document every failure before final submission.

\section*{Fresh Environment Protocol}\label{fresh-environment-protocol}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Fresh Environment Protocol}

\markright{Fresh Environment Protocol}

Complete these steps in a new Codespace or equivalent clean environment.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open the repository from GitHub.
\item
  Locate the project README and follow only the documented setup steps.
\item
  Render the Quarto book or report.
\item
  Run the NHANES dashboard from the repository root.
\item
  Open the Week 11 static dashboard source and render it as a standalone
  Quarto dashboard.
\item
  Check that all data paths are relative.
\item
  Record every failure, warning, unclear instruction, and repair.
\item
  Commit repairs with clear messages.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Failure Log}\label{failure-log}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Failure Log}

\markright{Failure Log}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Check & Result & Fix made & Owner \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Repository opens in fresh environment & & & \\
Packages install or load & & & \\
Report renders & & & \\
Dashboard runs locally & & & \\
Static dashboard renders & & & \\
README instructions are accurate & & & \\
Relative paths work & & & \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Peer Review Roles}\label{peer-review-roles}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Peer Review Roles}

\markright{Peer Review Roles}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Driver:} Runs the project exactly as documented.
\item
  \textbf{Navigator:} Reads instructions aloud and logs ambiguity.
\item
  \textbf{Auditor:} Checks paths, data provenance, citations, and AI-use
  notes.
\end{itemize}

Rotate roles after the first major failure or after 20 minutes.

\section*{Final Repair Checklist}\label{final-repair-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Final Repair Checklist}

\markright{Final Repair Checklist}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item[$\square$]
  All required outputs render or run from a fresh environment.
\item[$\square$]
  README instructions match the actual workflow.
\item[$\square$]
  Data paths are relative.
\item[$\square$]
  Cached data files needed for offline work are present.
\item[$\square$]
  Citations resolve.
\item[$\square$]
  AI-use notes identify what was drafted and how it was checked.
\item[$\square$]
  Remaining risks are documented in the failure log.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Assignment 4: Polyglot Awareness \& R
Deepening}\label{assignment-4-polyglot-awareness-r-deepening}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-3}

This assignment asks you to strengthen an R analysis first, then
translate a small piece of it to Python/pandas and verify that both
languages give the same descriptive answer. The goal is not to become a
Python expert. The goal is to become a careful bilingual reader who can
audit AI-assisted translation.

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-2}

Use the canonical NHANES case-study CSV:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If your notebook lives in \texttt{weeks/week05-polyglot-r-deepening/},
use:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Step-By-Step Tasks}\label{step-by-step-tasks}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create \texttt{assignments/assignment04-polyglot/submission/}.
\item
  In that folder, create \texttt{polyglot-parity.qmd}.
\item
  In R, load the NHANES CSV and produce a descriptive summary of BMI by
  \texttt{IncomeGroup} and \texttt{Gender}.
\item
  Refactor the R summary into one small function.
\item
  Create \texttt{translation.ipynb} and translate the same summary to
  Python/pandas.
\item
  Create \texttt{helpers.py} with at least one reusable Python helper
  and call it from the notebook.
\item
  Fill in a parity table comparing row counts, filtered row counts,
  grouping labels, and rounded summary values.
\item
  Add a short AI-use note describing what the tool helped with and how
  you verified the output.
\item
  Clear notebook outputs before committing.
\item
  Commit and sync your work.
\end{enumerate}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit}

Submit the following files in
\texttt{assignments/assignment04-polyglot/submission/}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{polyglot-parity.qmd}: your R analysis, R function, parity
  table, and AI-use note.
\item
  \texttt{translation.ipynb}: your Python translation notebook.
\item
  \texttt{helpers.py}: your reusable Python helper script.
\end{itemize}

Submission route: submit your repository link on Canvas after committing
and syncing the files above.

\section{Grading Checklist}\label{grading-checklist-1}

\chapter{Assignment 5: Visualization Audit and
Revision}\label{assignment-5-visualization-audit-and-revision}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-4}

Create a clear, honest health-data visualization from the NHANES Health
Equity classroom dataset. You will audit a flawed or AI-assisted plot,
revise it, and explain why the revision is more accurate and useful for
a non-technical audience.

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Worked example:
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/worked-example-ggplot.qmd}
\item
  Activity prompt:
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/activity-ai-visual-audit.qmd}
\end{itemize}

\section{Step-by-Step Tasks}\label{step-by-step-tasks-1}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Review the Week 6 worked example.
\item
  Choose one comparison from the NHANES Health Equity CSV snapshot.
\item
  Draft an initial plot using R and \texttt{ggplot2}.
\item
  Audit the plot for scale choices, labels, missing context, and
  overclaiming.
\item
  Revise the plot so it supports descriptive interpretation.
\item
  Write a caption and short audit note.
\item
  If AI helped you write or revise code, document what it did and how
  you checked it.
\end{enumerate}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit-1}

Place all submission files in:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{assignments/assignment05{-}visualization/submission/}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Submit these files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{visualization.qmd}: rerunnable Quarto file that loads the CSV
  with a relative path and creates the final plot.
\item
  \texttt{visualization.html}: rendered output.
\item
  \texttt{corrected-plot.png}: exported final plot.
\item
  \texttt{audit-note.md}: at least three issues you checked or
  corrected.
\item
  \texttt{caption.md}: final figure caption with data source, statistic,
  exclusions, and limitation.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md}: required if AI was used; otherwise include a
  one-sentence note saying AI was not used.
\end{itemize}

\section{Submission Route}\label{submission-route}

Submit a Canvas assignment entry that contains a link to the repository
folder \texttt{assignments/assignment05-visualization/submission/}.

\section{Grading Checklist}\label{grading-checklist-2}

Complete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Uses \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv} with
  a relative path.
\item
  Plot is rerunnable from the submitted Quarto file.
\item
  Title, axes, legend, and caption match the statistic shown.
\item
  Audit note identifies at least three meaningful issues.
\item
  Caption states that the result is descriptive and non-causal.
\item
  AI-use note is present and specific.
\end{itemize}

Needs work:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Uses an absolute or machine-specific path.
\item
  Plot title or caption overclaims causality or population inference.
\item
  Missing caption, audit note, or AI-use note.
\item
  Code does not run from the repository.
\item
  Revised plot is not materially different from the draft version.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Assignment 6: EDA, Table 1, and AI
Audit}\label{assignment-6-eda-table-1-and-ai-audit}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-5}

Produce a transparent descriptive EDA note and Table 1-style summary
from the NHANES Health Equity classroom dataset. You will also audit a
planted-error starter to practice catching correctness, reproducibility,
and interpretation problems.

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Worked example:
  \texttt{weeks/week07-eda-ai-audit/worked-example-table1.qmd}
\item
  Activity prompt:
  \texttt{weeks/week07-eda-ai-audit/activity-table1-audit.qmd}
\item
  Planted-error starter:
  \texttt{weeks/week07-eda-ai-audit/instructor/planted-error-starter.qmd}
\end{itemize}

\section{Step-by-Step Tasks}\label{step-by-step-tasks-2}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Load the CSV snapshot with a relative path.
\item
  Define and document an analysis cohort using age 20-80.
\item
  Create a missingness table for key variables.
\item
  Produce a Table 1-style summary by \texttt{IncomeGroup}.
\item
  Include N, BMI mean (SD), and age mean (SD).
\item
  Write a short methods note stating that the analysis is descriptive,
  unweighted, and non-causal.
\item
  Audit the planted-error starter and list at least five issues.
\item
  Categorize each issue as correctness, reproducibility, or
  interpretation.
\end{enumerate}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit-2}

Place all submission files in:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{assignments/assignment06{-}eda{-}ai{-}audit/submission/}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Submit these files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{eda-note.qmd}: rerunnable Quarto file with cohort definition,
  missingness table, Table 1, and methods note.
\item
  \texttt{eda-note.html}: rendered output.
\item
  \texttt{table1.csv}: exported Table 1 or equivalent descriptive
  summary.
\item
  \texttt{audit-note.md}: at least five planted issues with category,
  why it matters, and corrected approach.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md}: required if AI was used; otherwise include a
  one-sentence note saying AI was not used.
\end{itemize}

\section{Submission Route}\label{submission-route-1}

Submit a Canvas assignment entry that contains a link to the repository
folder \texttt{assignments/assignment06-eda-ai-audit/submission/}.

\section{Grading Checklist}\label{grading-checklist-3}

Complete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Uses \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv} with
  a relative path.
\item
  Cohort definition is stated before summarizing.
\item
  Missingness is checked and reported.
\item
  Table 1 includes N, BMI mean (SD), and age mean (SD) by group.
\item
  Methods note avoids causal or population-level overclaims.
\item
  Audit note identifies at least five issues across correctness,
  reproducibility, and interpretation.
\item
  AI-use note is present and specific.
\end{itemize}

Needs work:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Uses an absolute or machine-specific path.
\item
  Cohort is unclear or inconsistent with the code.
\item
  Rows are dropped silently without missingness documentation.
\item
  Statistics are mislabeled or missing variability measures.
\item
  Interpretation overclaims causality.
\item
  Code does not render from the repository.
\end{itemize}
